For example, Block and colleagues (2022) found that brief stereotype-relevant stories changed children's math-gender stereotypes but did not change boys' or girls' own identification with math. Similarly, a small study (N = 82) found that viewing pictures of children engaging with a counter-stereotypical toy reduced children's stereotypes about toys but did not affect children's own toy preferences (Spinner et al., 2018). Another recent study exposed 125 Dutch children to counter-stereotypical women heroines and found reductions in gender stereotypes about specific activities but not in children's own communal values or career interests (Endendijk, 2025). It remains an open question whether the null effects of these prior interventions on children's own values and career interests are genuine or reflect insufficient statistical power-a question that a larger, adequately powered study such as ours may help to answer.

Beyond these methodological issues, prior work has concentrated on a narrow set of domains. In particular, role-model research has focused overwhelmingly on high-status, agentic fields such as STEM (Gladstone & Cimpian, 2021; Morgenroth et al., 2015). Communion differs from these domains in theoretically important ways: It is stereotypically feminine, lower in perceived status, and oriented toward caring for others rather than individual achievement. It is therefore not obvious whether findings from STEM role-model research will transfer.

Although role models have often been discussed as a promising avenue for increasing boys' and men's endorsement of communal values and interest in communal careers (Meeussen et al., 2020; Olsson & Martiny, 2018)

Communion

In the case of communion, gender stereotypes are especially intertwined with perceptions about societal status. Adults consistently associate communal values and communal traits with low status and power (Conway et al., 1996; Eagly & Steffen, 1984; Fiske et al., 2002), whereas agentic values explicitly include striving for power, status, and achievement (Trapnell & Paulhus, 2012). Similarly, communal professions, such as nurse or teacher, are assigned relatively low societal status compared to more agentic professions that require similar levels of education, such as computer programmer (Block, Croft, et al., 2018; Cejka & Eagly, 1999). Such low-status perceptions about communion exacerbate gender differences in communion: These perceptions tend to deter men, over and above communion's association with women (Brescoll et al., 2012; Croft et al., 2015; Moss-Racusin, 2014; Moss-Racusin et al., 2010). In light of this work, it may be necessary to increase the status of communion-not just change gender stereotypes about communion-in order to encourage boys to pursue communal roles.

By late childhood and adolescence, children's beliefs about the status of communion begin to mirror those of adults. Like adults, elementary school children assign relatively low social status to communal careers that are usually dominated by women (Liben et al., 2001; Teig & Susskind, 2008). Similarly, 6-to 8-year-olds assign lower status to novel occupations that are described as dominated by women rather than men (Liben et al., 2001), although notably the relevant studies did not portray the woman-dominated occupations as communal. This effect was stronger in older children. In parallel, there is mixed evidence on whether school-aged children also hold status or power stereotypes about gender itself (rather than communal occupations):

While some studies find that boys link power to their gender more than girls do (Reyes-Jaquez & Koenig, 2022), others show mixed effects depending on the measure used (Meng et al., 2024).

Taken together, these findings suggest that beliefs about communion, gender, and status may be in flux during the transition from preschool to elementary school.

As in adults, the low social status tied to communal roles has been implicated in gender differences in children's interest in such roles. Experimentally manipulated status cues (salary and power) have been shown to affect both girls' and boys' interest in jobs (Olsen et al., 2023), but some studies also suggest that these cues are particularly powerful among boys (Hayes et al., 2018). In a large survey of adolescents' career aspirations, boys prioritized the status of careers more than girls did (Korlat et al., 2023). Crucially, these gender differences were tied to agentic and communal self-views: Adolescents who saw themselves as more agentic tended to favor high-status careers, whereas those with stronger communal self-views gravitated toward communal careers, which were often lower in status.

These findings suggest a clear prediction for the present intervention: If boys' lower interest in communal careers is driven, at least in part, by the low perceived status of these careers, then pairing communal careers with high-status cues should increase boys' receptiveness and strengthen the intervention's effects. To signal status, we focus on wealth, which is among the most concrete and developmentally accessible markers of status for children in this age range (e.g., Enright et al., 2020; Shutts et al., 2016). Thus, in the present study, wealth served as our operationalization of the broader construct of status.

It is unclear, however, whether wealth-based cues embedded in brief role-model portrayals can effectively shape elementary school-aged children's own values and career preferences. Although some evidence suggests that children use status cues, including wealth, when forming social (Enright et al., 2020) and career (Olsen et al., 2023) preferences from a young age, other evidence suggests that these links are weak or inconsistent, particularly when they concern gender-relevant preferences (e.g., Mandalaywala et al., 2020). This may be because gender is an especially salient and self-relevant social category in childhood (Shutts, 2015); even when children detect status cues, they may still rely on gender stereotypes when judging which values and careers are desirable and appropriate for themselves. Given these nuanced prior findings, it is important to test whether boys and girls use status cues when exposed to counterstereotypic communal role models. If children use these cues to inform their values and career interests, then adding them to counter-stereotypic role models might amplify the intervention's effects; if not, the cues may have little impact. We tested these alternative possibilities by embedding wealth-based status cues in the counter-stereotypic role-model vignettes.

The Present Research

The present preregistered experiment tested whether a brief, double-masked, 1 videobased role model intervention could reduce children's gender stereotypes about communion and agency and whether it could also narrow gender differences in children's own values and career 1 Both the children and the experimenters were unaware of condition.

interests. We recruited 7-to 10-year-old children because this is a promising developmental window for such an intervention: Gender stereotypes about communion are well established by this age and already associated with children's own values (Block et al., 2025), yet children's beliefs and interests remain malleable. We further tested whether adding status cues would strengthen the intervention's effects, reasoning that the low perceived status of communal roles is a potential obstacle to boys' pursuit of such roles. Whether such cues would help at this age, however, was unclear: Although children this age detect and use wealth cues in some contexts (Olsen et al., 2023), gender is a highly salient social category in childhood (Mandalaywala et al., 2020; Shutts, 2015) and may overshadow status information when children judge what is appropriate for themselves.

Children were randomly assigned to one of three role-model conditions: "stereotypical" (girls portrayed as communal and entering communal careers; boys portrayed as agentic and entering agentic careers), "counter-stereotypical" (these pairings reversed), or "counterstereotypical + status" (the counter-stereotypical vignettes with added wealth cues signaling occupational success). After viewing six animated vignettes, children reported their gender stereotypes about communal and agentic values and careers, as well as their own endorsement of these values and interest in these careers.

Our preregistered hypotheses were organized around three questions. First, we examined effects on children's beliefs about the social world. We hypothesized that children exposed to counter-stereotypical exemplars would report weaker gender stereotypes about communal values and communal careers than children in the stereotypical condition. By observing boys enacting communal roles and girls enacting agentic ones, children should update their beliefs about how gender-bound these domains are.

Second, we examined downstream consequences for children's own values and career interests. Drawing on theory and evidence on how stereotypes constrain self-relevant processes, we predicted that exposure to counter-stereotypical exemplars would mitigate gender gaps in children's own values and career interests. Specifically, we hypothesized that the counterstereotypical conditions would show smaller gender differences in children's endorsement of communal values and their interest in communal careers (i.e., we would observe condition × gender interactions), driven primarily by boys in these conditions reporting stronger communal values and career interests than boys in the stereotypical condition.

Third, we tested whether status cues amplify intervention effects. If the low perceived status of communal careers is an additional barrier to boys' pursuit of such careers-beyond the association between communion and girls and women-then adding high-status cues to counterstereotypical exemplars should strengthen the intervention's impact. We held this prediction more tentatively, however. Although children at these ages are sensitive to wealth-based status cues (Legaspi et al., 2023; Olsen et al., 2023), gender looms unusually large in childhood (Shutts, 2015), raising the possibility that the gendered content of our vignettes might simply override any signal carried by wealth.

Finally, because our design reversed role pairings for both genders, we tested parallel hypotheses for agency: that counter-stereotypical exemplars would weaken stereotypes linking agency with men and boys, and reduce gender differences in children's own agentic values and career interests. While our primary theoretical focus is on boys' low endorsement of communal values and low interest in communal careers, these parallel tests allowed us to examine the intervention's breadth. We also preregistered exploratory analyses testing whether the effects of the intervention varied across the age range from 7 to 10 years.

Method Open and Transparent Scientific Practices

The sample size, exact procedures, hypotheses, and a detailed analysis plan were preregistered publicly on the Open Science Framework (OSF) (link anonymized for review) (https://osf.io/c7pfw/overview?view_only=7ede5291b2a9415686272f9bdf72da2a). This We conducted an a priori power analysis in G*Power 3.1 (Faul et al., 2009) to determine the sample size that would provide 80% power to detect a small-to medium-sized interaction between condition and gender (Cohen's f ~ 0.20). We chose to power the study to detect this key interaction because interactions are harder to detect than main effects (e.g., McClelland & Judd, 1993); thus, powering for this interaction should generally provide adequate power to detect main effects of similar or larger magnitude. The power analysis suggested a final sample size of approximately 248 children.

Seventeen additional children were tested but excluded from analyses based on preregistered criteria: not completing the dependent variables (n = 5), Experimenter 2 accidentally becoming aware of the experimental condition before their part of the study (see below; n = 4), technical issues (n = 4), lack of English comprehension (n = 3), or failing at least two comprehension checks (see below; n = 1). Because more children met exclusion criteria than anticipated, our final analyzable sample (N = 244) fell 4 short of the preregistered target of 248 -a minor deviation from the preregistration. A sensitivity analysis (G*Power 3.1; Faul et al., 2009) confirmed that N = 244 still provided approximately 80% power to detect the targeted interaction effect (Cohen's f ~ 0.20).

We asked parents to report the racial and ethnic identity of their children. Of the total sample, 91.4% parents (223 of 244) reported their children's racial identity. Of these, 65.0% of children were identified as White, 14.3% as Asian, 4.9% as Black or African American, and 15.2% as multiracial. In addition, 91.8% of parents (224/244) reported their children's ethnic identity. Of these 12.5% were identified as Hispanic and 87.5% as non-Hispanic.

Procedure

Each child participated individually, guided through the procedure via the videoconferencing software Zoom by two experimenters specifically trained to work with children.

The Zoom call began with informing the children's legal guardians about the topic and general procedure and ensuring consent. Children were then given the opportunity to assent to participation and assured they could stop at any time. At the start of the experimental procedure, the first experimenter placed the second experimenter in the waiting room to ensure the latter remained unaware of experimental condition.

Children were randomly assigned to one of three video exemplar conditions: stereotypical, counter-stereotypical, and counter-stereotypical + status. Each condition included six animated videos of cartoon human characters. Following each pair of videos, children completed two attention checks to ensure they listened to and understood the content of the stories. The length of the intervention was informed by prior exemplar-based interventions (Block et al., 2022; Gonzalez et al., 2017) and by our lab's experience with the amount of material children can attend to in online experimental sessions.

After completing the video portion of the study, children were thanked and asked if they wanted to participate in what was described as "another study" but, in reality, was our battery of dependent variables. This framing was used to minimize potential demand characteristics (e.g., children treating the next questions as a test of the videos or trying to give "expected" answers).

The second experimenter, who was unaware of which condition had been run in the first part (to further limit demand characteristics), then entered the Zoom meeting to complete all dependent variables with the children without the first experimenter, using visual aids displayed on the shared screen and recording their responses.

Intervention Materials

In each of the three conditions (stereotypical, counter-stereotypical, and counterstereotypical + status), children viewed three pairs of videos, each featuring a boy and girl who displayed communal or agentic values and roles (see OSF repository for sample videos and a detailed overview of the script for the videos across conditions). For each video, the child protagonist valued either communality or agency and, as an adult, pursued either a communal (i.e., nurse, teacher, psychologist) or agentic (i.e., architect, manager, judge) career, matching the values displayed by their childhood character. For example, a participant in the stereotypical condition would see three girls who valued communality and pursued communal careers as adults and three boys who valued agency and pursued agentic careers as adults (presented in a random order). Each of the six stories began with the narrator describing the character as similar in age to the participant and highlighting the character's values (communal or agentic, depending on condition). Accompanying animations of the character illustrated these values (e.g., the character who valued helping was shown helping another child up an obstacle). The narrator then described the character as an adult who still holds the same values and is now in a valuecongruent career (e.g., nursing for communal values, lawyer for agentic values). Additionally, the narrator emphasized that the character enjoyed their work in all conditions. Animations again accompanied these descriptions, showing the characters grown up and in their jobs.

To ensure standardization, animated videos were created with Vyond software, and the narration was pre-recorded by researchers with a strict script (a man researcher for boy characters, a woman researcher for girl characters). To enhance relatability, the children in the vignettes represented the three largest ethnic groups in the participant pool: White, Black, and East Asian. The careers with which the ethnicities were paired were randomized between participants. Figure 1 shows still frames and the accompanying script from a sample video from each of the three conditions.

We chose the careers featured in the videos based on a pilot study. We began with a pool of 16 careers chosen because prior work suggested that children in our age range hold strong gender stereotypes about them (Weisgram et al., 2010; Block et al., 2026). These 16 careers were then included in a small pilot study (n = 53 children, ages 5 to 10 years, 23 girls) to identify which careers children most strongly associated with communal versus agentic values. For each of the 16 careers in the pilot, children were asked to indicate whether people in this career cared more about "helping" (i.e., communion) or "making decisions" (i.e., agency). For our main study, we selected the four careers from this pilot with the highest communal ratings and the four careers with the highest agentic ratings. Three of these careers per career-type were then used in the videos and in our measures; the fourth was used only in our measures (see below).

Stereotypical Condition

In the stereotypical condition, children viewed three videos about boys who valued agentic traits (e.g., being the best) and engaged in agentic activities (e.g., winning a medal). As adults, these characters entered stereotypically masculine agentic careers (e.g., manager). Paired with each boy's video, there were three videos about girls who valued communal traits (e.g., being kind to other people) and engaged in communal activities (e.g., giving someone flowers).

As adults, these girls entered stereotypically feminine communal careers (e.g., nurse).

Counter-Stereotypical Condition

In the counter-stereotypical condition, children saw the same characters, values, and careers, but the role/gender pairings were reversed: Boys exhibited communal values, actions, and career paths, while girls exhibited agentic values, actions, and career paths.

Counter-Stereotypical + Status Condition

In the counter-stereotypical + status condition, children viewed the same six videos as in the counter-stereotypical condition, except that the narrator also explained that each character was successful at work and could afford expensive items (e.g., houses, cars). The animations displayed images indicating wealth, chosen based on research showing that children in our age range understand money and possessions as indicators of high status (Shutts et al., 2016). Note. The videos illustrated here all display communal characters for comparison between conditions, but each condition contained an equal number of videos about agentic characters.

The videos in each condition depicted two White, two Black, and two East Asian characters (one per video, for a total of six videos). The careers with which the ethnicities were paired were randomized between participants. The key elements of the script (specifically, information about communality and wealth) are bolded; these elements varied across videos (communality vs. agency) or conditions (wealth vs. not).

Measures

After watching the six videos, children answered questions about each of our 4 variables of interest: (a) gender stereotypes about values, (b) gender stereotypes about careers, (c) own values, and (d) own career interests, all of which were asked with respect to both agency and communality. The questions pertaining to values (values stereotypes, own values) and the questions about careers (career stereotypes, own career interest) were each presented as a block, and the order of these blocks was randomized. Within these two blocks (namely, values and careers), the questions about stereotypes and the questions about the self were also each presented as a block, and the order of these blocks was randomized. Within these sub-blocks, the questions pertaining to communality and agency were interspersed and presented in a random order with only one question displayed on the screen at a time.

Gender Stereotypes about Values.

Children reported their gender stereotypes about six communal values (being kind to other people, doing nice things for others, being nice to other people, making other people happy, thinking about other people's feelings, helping other people) and six agentic values (making decisions, winning, being the best, making choices about what to do, being the boss, being in charge). For each value, children were asked, "Who do you think cares about [value] more?" Children chose one of five answer options: girls care a lot more, girls care a little more, girls and boys care the same amount, boys care a little more, boys care a lot more. These answer options were accompanied by colored circles (blue for boy options, red for girl options, gray for gender-neutral option) that varied in size based on the extremity of the answer (see Figure S1 in the Supplementary Online Materials

Gender Stereotypes about Careers.

Similarly, children were asked to report their gender stereotypes regarding four communal careers (nurse, teacher, psychologist, speech therapist) and four agentic careers (architect, manager, judge, lawyer), chosen based on the aforementioned pilot study. The careers consisted of the three featured in the vignettes, plus an additional communal and agentic career. These additional careers were included to examine whether children would generalize the intervention message to novel careers. Each career was accompanied by a brief description (see Supplementary Note S1 in the SOM). After this initial description, children were asked, "Who do you think wants to be a [career] more when they grow up?" Children were given five answer options: girls want to be [career] a lot more, girls want to be [career] a little more, girls and boys want to be [career] the same amount, boys want to be [career] a little more, boys want to be [career] a lot more (see Figure S2 in the SOM). Whether girl or boy answer options were presented first was again randomized within and between participants. Responses were coded such that higher values represented more stereotypical responses. That is, for communal career stereotypes, responses were coded on a 5-point scale ranging from2 = girls want to be [career] a lot more to -2 = boys want to be [career] a lot more, with 0 representing no gender stereotype about communal careers. For agentic career stereotypes, responses were coded on a 5-point scale ranging from2 = boys want to be [career] a lot more to -2 = girls want to be [career] a lot more, with 0 representing no gender stereotype about agentic careers. Responses were averaged to represent children's stereotypes regarding communal (α = 0.54) and agentic (α = 0.53) careers.

Given the modest scale reliabilities, we examined whether some subset of the careers formed a more reliable composite. However, we found similar correlations among all careers within career types. Additionally, a parallel analysis (Horn, 1965) on the communal and, separately, the agentic items suggested retaining just one component, with all items loading similarly onto that component (see Tables S13 and S14 in the SOM). As a result, we opted to follow our preregistered analysis plan and averaged all communal and, separately, all agentic items into their respective composite variables.

Own Values.

Children reported how much they personally cared about the same six communal values (e.g., being kind to other people) and six agentic values (e.g., making decisions) from the measure of gender stereotypes about communal and agentic values. For each value, children were asked, "How important do you think it is to [value]?" Children responded on a 5-point scale ranging from1 = not very important to5 = super important (see Figure S3 in the SOM). Responses were averaged to represent children's own communal (α = 0.85) and agentic (α = 0.85) values.

Own Career Interests.

Children also reported their personal interest in the same four communal and four agentic careers. For each career, children were asked, "When you are a grown-up, how much do you want to be a [career]?" Children responded on a 4-point scale ranging from1 = not want to at all to4 = really want to. Response options were accompanied by visual aids to facilitate children's responding: The lowest option (not want at all) accompanied by a red X, and the other three options were accompanied by increasingly large green check marks (see Figure S4 in the SOM). Responses were averaged to represent children' own communal (α = .70) and agentic (α = .57) career interests.

Given the modest reliability of the measure of agentic career interests, we examined whether some subset of these careers formed a more reliable composite. However, as for the career stereotypes, we found similar correlations among all agentic careers. Additionally, a parallel analysis (Horn, 1965) suggested retaining just one component, with all items loading similarly onto that component (see Table S15 in the SOM). As a result, we opted to follow our preregistered analysis plan and averaged all agentic career interest items into a composite variable.

Results

Data Processing

Following our preregistered plan, we winsorized any values that fell more than 3 SDs above or below the sample mean on each composite variable (3 for communal values stereotypes, 2 for agentic values stereotypes, 2 for communal career stereotypes, 2 for agentic career stereotypes, and 2 for own communal values). The winsorized values came from 7 participants. Correlations among all outcome variables, separated by participant gender, can be found in Table 1. Means by gender and condition can be found in Table 2.

Predictions and Analytic Strategy

Preregistered Analyses

For stereotypes, we hypothesized that there would be a main effect of condition such that children in the stereotypical condition would respond more stereotypically than children in the two intervention conditions. For children's own values and interests, we hypothesized that there would be an interaction between child gender and condition such that children in the intervention conditions would have smaller gender differences than children in the stereotypical condition.

We preregistered two main analyses: (a) one where each dependent variable was predicted by condition and child gender with a covariate for children's age and (b) another in which there was an additional interaction between condition and child gender. We also preregistered a model with a two-way interaction between condition and child age to test for agerelated changes in condition effects. For simplicity, we report the results (including the main effects) from a model with all terms of interest: condition, child gender, child age, condition × child gender, and condition × child age. For each model, condition was coded as an unordered three-level factor (stereotypical, counter-stereotypical, counter-stereotypical + status), child gender was a dichotomous variable (1 = girl,0 = boy), and children's age was a continuous variable (in years with 2+ decimal precision, mean-centered).

All analyses were conducted using the statistical software R (R Core Team, 2021). For significant effects, we calculated marginal means and tests using the package emmeans (Lenth, 2023). We adjusted for multiple comparisons using the Tukey HSD method. Tables S1-S4 in the SOM contain all model output.

As a measure of effect size, we additionally provide standardized mean differences. These were estimated as coefficients in regression models in which we standardized the sole continuous predictor (age) and the dependent variable. With this scaling, the pairwise comparisons between conditions or gender groups can be interpreted as the fraction of a standard deviation (SD) difference between those groups, an approximate equivalent to Cohen's ds (e.g., Robinson & Lubienski, 2011).

Exploratory, Non-Preregistered Analysis: Combining the Two Intervention Conditions

In our preregistration, we hypothesized that the intervention effects might be stronger in the counter-stereotypical + status condition than in the counter-stereotypical condition; however, children's responses to these two conditions were demonstrably similar (see results that follow).

Therefore, we also briefly report an exploratory, non-preregistered analysis in which we collapse these two intervention conditions, which increases our power to detect an intervention effect by approximately 10 percentage points for effects of the magnitude observed here. In this model, the condition variable is now dichotomous (1 = intervention,0 = stereotypical), and both condition and child gender are mean-centered to facilitate interpretation of lower-order effects (e.g., Muradoglu et al., 2023). Tables S5-S8 in the SOM list all model output.

Counter-Stereotypical Exemplars Reduce Children's Gender Stereotypes about Values

Gender Stereotypes about Communal Values

Preregistered Analyses (Separate Intervention Conditions).

In line with our hypotheses, children in the stereotypical condition reported stronger gender stereotypes about communal values (M = 0.56) than children in the counter-stereotypical (M = 0.22) or counterstereotypical + status (M = 0.32) conditions (main effect of condition: F(2, 235) = 7.26, p < .001; see Figure 2A and Table S1). Children in the stereotypical condition reported 0.57 SD more stereotypical responses than children in the counter-stereotypical condition (b = 0.34, SE = 0.09, p = .001) and 0.40 SD more stereotypical responses than children in the counter-stereotypical + status condition (b = 0.24, SE = 0.09, p = .020). There was no significant difference in children's gender stereotypes about communal values between the two intervention conditions (p = .51).

Additionally, girls (M = 0.51) reported overall stronger gender stereotypes about communal values than did boys (M = 0.22; main effect of gender: F(1, 235) = 17.18, p < .001).

This amounted to a 0.50 SD gender difference in gender stereotypes about communal values.

There was no main effect of children's age, no interaction between child gender and condition, and no interaction between child age and condition (ps ≥ .10).

Exploratory Analyses (Combined Intervention Conditions).

In the exploratory, nonpreregistered analysis with the combined intervention conditions, the pattern of results was the same as above (see Table S5).

Gender Stereotypes about Agentic Values

Preregistered Analyses (Separate Intervention Conditions).

In line with our hypotheses, children in the stereotypical condition reported stronger gender stereotypes about agentic values (M = 0.52) than children in the counter-stereotypical (M = 0.10) or counterstereotypical + status (M = 0.22) conditions (main effect of condition: F(2, 235) = 10.50, p < .001; see Figure 2B and Table S1). Children in the stereotypical condition reported 0.70 SD more stereotypical responses than children in the counter-stereotypical condition (b = 0.42, SE = 0.09, p < .001) and 0.50 SD more stereotypical responses than children in the counter-stereotypical + status condition (b = 0.30, SE = 0.09, p = .004). There was no significant difference in children's gender stereotypes about agentic values between the two intervention conditions (p = .38).

Finally, there were no main effects of child gender or child age, nor was there an interaction between child gender and condition (ps ≥ .35). The model did reveal, however, a significant interaction between child age and condition, which we discuss in more detail in a subsequent section alongside all analogous interactions across models: Generally, the intervention seemed somewhat more effective among younger children.

Exploratory Analyses (Combined Intervention Conditions).

In the exploratory, nonpreregistered analysis with the combined intervention conditions, the pattern of results was the same as above (see Table S5). Note. Panel A plots children's gender stereotypes about communal values. Panel B plots children's gender stereotypes about agentic values. Large shapes are predicted means; error bars are 95% confidence intervals; small shapes are horizontally-jittered individual data points; the dashed horizontal line represents no gender stereotype about values. *p < .05, **p < .01, ***p < .001 Counter-Stereotypical Exemplars Reduce Children's Gender Stereotypes about Careers

Gender Stereotypes about Communal Careers

Preregistered Analyses (Separate Intervention Conditions).

In line with our hypotheses, children in the stereotypical condition reported stronger gender stereotypes about communal careers (M = 0.62) than children in the counter-stereotypical (M = 0.32) or counterstereotypical + status (M = 0.33) conditions (main effect of condition: F(2, 235) = 6.94, p = .001; see Figure 3A and Table S2). Children in the stereotypical condition reported 0.54 SD more stereotypical responses about communal careers than did children in the counter-stereotypical condition (b = 0.30, SE = 0.09, p = .002) and 0.52 SD more stereotypical responses than did children in the counter-stereotypical + status condition (b = 0.29, SE = 0.09, p = .003). There was no significant difference in children's gender stereotypes about communal careers between the two intervention conditions (p = .99). Finally, there were no main effects of child gender or child age, nor was there an interaction between child gender and condition (ps ≥ .63).

Exploratory Analyses (Combined Intervention Conditions).

In the exploratory, nonpreregistered analysis with the combined intervention conditions, the pattern of results was the same as above (see Table S6). Note. Panel A plots children's gender stereotypes about communal careers. Panel B plots children's gender stereotypes about agentic careers. Large shapes are predicted means; error bars are 95% confidence intervals; small shapes are horizontally-jittered individual data points; the dashed horizontal line represents no gender stereotype about values. *p < .05, **p < .01, ***p < .001

Gender Stereotypes about Agentic Careers

Preregistered Analyses (Separate Intervention Conditions).

In line with our hypotheses, children in the stereotypical condition reported stronger gender stereotypes about agentic careers (M = 0.57) than children in the counter-stereotypical (M = 0.09) or counterstereotypical + status (M = 0.13) conditions (main effect of condition: F(2, 235) = 19.07, p < .001; see Figure 3B and Table S2). Children in the stereotypical condition reported 0.82 SD more stereotypical responses than children in the counter-stereotypical condition (b = 0.48, SE = 0.09, p < .001) and 0.75 SD more stereotypical responses than children in the counter-stereotypical + status condition (b = 0.44, SE = 0.09, p < .001). There was no significant difference in children's gender stereotypes about agentic careers between the two intervention conditions (p = .88).

Finally, there were no main effects of child gender or child age, nor was there an interaction between child gender and condition (ps > .057).

Exploratory Analyses (Combined Intervention Conditions).

In the exploratory, nonpreregistered analysis with the combined intervention conditions, the pattern of results was the same as above (see Table S6).

Counter-Stereotypical Exemplars May Reduce Gender Differences in Values

Children's Own Communal Values

Preregistered Analyses (Separate Intervention Conditions).

Older children reported lower communal values than younger children, b = -0.09, SE = 0.03, p = .003. There were no significant main effects of condition, F(2, 235) = 0.03, p = .97, or child gender, F(1, 235) = 0.93, p = .34, on children's own communal values, nor was there an interaction between the two, F(2, 235) = 2.85, p = .060 (see Table S3).

Exploratory Analyses (Combined Intervention Conditions).

In the exploratory, nonpreregistered analyses with the combined intervention conditions, the hypothesized interaction between condition and child gender was significant (see Figure 4, as well as Table S7 and Supplementary Note S2 in the SOM). In the stereotypical condition, girls reported significantly greater communal values than boys, whereas this group difference was non-significant in the combined counter-stereotypical condition. Note. Large shapes are predicted means; error bars are standard errors; small shapes are horizontally jittered individual data points. *p < .05

Children's Own Agentic Values

Preregistered Analyses (Separate Intervention Conditions).

Older children reported greater agentic values than younger children, b = 0.14, SE = 0.05, p = .005. There was no main effect of condition, F(2, 235) = 1.19, p = .31, or child gender, F(1, 235) = 0.25, p = .62, nor was there an interaction between the two, F(2, 235) = 1.63, p = .20 (see Table S3).

Exploratory Analyses (Combined Intervention Conditions).

In the exploratory, nonpreregistered analysis with the combined intervention conditions, the pattern of results was the same as above (see Table S7 and Supplementary Note S2).

Counter-Stereotypical Exemplars May Reduce Gender Differences in Career Interests

Interest in Communal Careers

Preregistered Analyses (Separate Intervention Conditions).

Girls (M = 2.36) reported more interest in communal careers than did boys (M = 1.92), F(1, 235) = 24.86, p < .001 (see Table S4). This amounted to a 0.62 SD difference. Additionally, older children reported less interest in communal careers than did younger children, b = -0.13, SE = 0.04, p = .001. There was no main effect of condition on children's interest in communal careers, F(2, 235) = 0.28, p = .75, nor a significant interaction between condition and child gender, F(2, 235) = 2.89, p = .058.

Exploratory Analyses (Combined Intervention Conditions).

In the exploratory, nonpreregistered analysis with the combined intervention conditions, the predicted interaction between condition and child gender was significant (see Figure 5, as well as Table S8 and Supplementary Note S2 in the SOM). In the stereotypical condition, girls reported significantly more interest in communal careers than boys did, whereas this gender gap was reduced but still significant in the combined counter-stereotypic conditions. Note. Large shapes are predicted means; error bars are standard errors; small shapes are horizontally jittered individual data points. *p < .05, **p < .01, ***p < .001

Interest in Agentic Careers

Preregistered Analyses (Separate Intervention Conditions).

We observed a main effect of condition, F(2, 235) = 5.44, p = .005 (see Table S4). Children in the counterstereotypical + status condition (M = 2.22) reported 0.49 SD more interest in agentic careers than children in the stereotypical condition (M = 1.90, b = 0.32, SE = 0.10, p = .006). There was no significant difference in children's interest in agentic careers between the stereotypical and counter-stereotypical (M = 2.10) conditions or the two intervention conditions (ps ≥ .14). There were no main effects of child gender or age, nor was there an interaction between child gender and condition (ps ≥ .18).

Exploratory Analyses (Combined Intervention Conditions).

In the exploratory, nonpreregistered analysis with the combined intervention conditions, the pattern of results was the same as above (see Table S8 and Supplementary Note S2).

Counter-Stereotypical Exemplars May Be Particularly Effective Among Young Children

We preregistered that we would explore whether the intervention's effects were stronger among younger or older children. Age moderation was generally weak, but a suggestive pattern emerged for the stereotype outcome: The intervention's effects were largest among younger children. This pattern reached significance for gender stereotypes about agentic values (p = .023; Table S1) and, when comparing the stereotypical condition against the combined intervention conditions, for gender stereotypes about agentic careers (p = .029; Table S6). In both cases, the gap between conditions was wider among younger participants. A similar but non-significant pattern emerged for gender stereotypes about communal values and careers (ps ≤ .10; Tables S1, S2, S5, and S6). Age did not moderate the intervention's effects on children's own values or career interests (ps > .30).

No Evidence that Intervention Effects Vary by Career

Given the modest reliability of the career scales, we explored whether averaging across careers obscured meaningful career-specific variation in the intervention effects for either children's career stereotypes or their own career interests. Specifically, we fit non-preregistered linear mixed-effects models to the individual career ratings that paralleled our primary models but also included career (a four-level categorical variable) and its interactions. Because the outcomes were modeled at item level rather than as a composite, the models included a random intercept for participant to account for the non-independence of a participant's responses. We fit these models separately for communal and agentic career stereotypes and interests, first treating condition as a three-level factor and then repeating the analyses with the two intervention conditions combined, paralleling our exploratory analyses above. With the three-level condition factor, there were no significant career × condition interactions (ps > .34) or career × condition × gender interactions (ps > .09; see Tables S16 and S18 in the SOM). The same was true when the intervention conditions were combined (see Tables S17 and S19 in the SOM). We thus found no evidence of heterogeneity in the key condition effects across the careers included in the study.

Condition Effects Mostly Generalize to Careers Not Featured in the Intervention

The preceding analyses tested for heterogeneity across individual careers. We next asked a distinct question: whether intervention effects were specifically stronger for careers that children had encountered in the intervention (i.e., nurse, teacher, psychologist, architect, manager, judge) than for the communal and agentic careers presented only at test (speech therapist and lawyer, respectively). If the intervention shifted children's stereotypes and preferences about careers more generally, rather than only about the specific careers they heard about in the intervention materials, the effects should generalize. Note that this analysis tests a specific, theoretically motivated contrast (featured vs. non-featured), which can be sensitive to differences that the preceding omnibus test across all four careers is not.

To answer this question, we conducted four non-preregistered linear mixed-effects models with gender stereotypes about (a) communal or (b) agentic careers or interest in (c) communal or (d) agentic careers as an outcome. The predictors were the same as in the main analyses, except we also added whether the career had previously been featured in the intervention materials (1 = featured;0 = not featured) and its interactions with condition, child gender, and child age. Again, because the outcomes were modeled at item level rather than as a composite, the models included a random intercept for participant to account for the nonindependence of a participant's responses. We ran these models with condition both as a threelevel variable (see Tables S9 and S11), consistent with the main modeling strategy in our preregistration, and as a dichotomous variable (1 = intervention,0 = stereotypical; see Tables S10 and S12), consistent with the combined-conditions exploratory analyses reported above.

Significant interactions between condition and the featured/non-featured variable would indicate that the intervention was differentially effective for careers that had been featured in the intervention materials versus those included as a generalization test.

When condition was modeled as a three-level variable, there were no significant interactions between condition and whether careers had been featured in the intervention (vs. not) on any outcome, all ps > .084. Thus, these models provided little evidence that intervention effects differed between featured and non-featured careers. When condition was modeled as a dichotomous variable, only one out of the four possible two-way condition × featured/nonfeatured interactions was significant, for gender stereotypes about communal careers (b = -0.26, SE = 0.13, p = .038; all other ps > .78). The combined interventions reduced children's gender stereotypes about the communal careers featured in the intervention by 0.41 SD (Ms = 0.72 and 0.37 for the stereotypical and the combined intervention conditions, respectively; b = 0.36, SE = 0.08, p < .001), whereas the equivalent reduction for the non-featured communal career was only 0.11 SD (Ms = 0.31 and 0.22 for the stereotypical and the combined intervention conditions, respectively; b = 0.10, SE = 0.12, p = .43).

Taken together, these analyses indicate that the intervention's effects extended beyond the specific careers children saw: Across most outcomes, condition effects were comparable for featured and non-featured careers, consistent with the possibility that children updated broader beliefs and attitudes about communal and agentic careers rather than simply encoding information about specific jobs. The one exception was the variable measuring children's gender stereotypes about communal careers, for which the reduction was larger for featured careers (0.41 SD) than for the single non-featured communal career (0.11 SD). Given that this was the only significant interaction among the four tested (or eight, considering the analyses with the three-level condition variable), we interpret it cautiously. Nevertheless, it suggests that transfer to gender stereotypes about unfamiliar communal careers may be more limited and warrants further study.

Discussion

Despite societal progress toward gender equity, communion remains highly gendered (Croft et al., 2015; Van Laar et al., 2024). Indeed, gender stereotypes about communion are especially strong in Western countries (Kosakowska-Berezecka et al., 2025; Napp, 2023) and have become stronger over time (Eagly et al., 2020). Given the constraining effects that stereotypes have on children (Bian et al., 2017; Block et al., 2025; Cvencek et al., 2015) In line with the preregistered predictions, children who viewed counter-stereotypical exemplars (namely, boys and men in communal roles and girls and women in agentic roles) reported weaker gender stereotypes than children who had seen videos about stereotypical exemplars. This effect was consistent across stereotypes about communal and agentic values and careers. Further, these effects were similar in magnitude for boys and girls, suggesting children of both genders were sensitive to this type of intervention. As such, the current study supports the power that counter-stereotypical role models have in changing gender stereotypes about communion and agency, extending past work that had focused on the reduction of racial biases (Gonzalez et al., 2016 (Gonzalez et al., , 2017 (Gonzalez et al., , 2021) ) and gender-math stereotypes (Block et al., 2022). Taken together, these findings suggest that relatively simple, video-based role-model interventions can shift children's beliefs about who values communion and agency and who belongs in communal and agentic careers. These changes in gendered beliefs occurred after a single, short session conducted online, underscoring both the malleability of children's stereotypes and the potential scalability of this approach. Also notable, the effects were not limited to the specific careers that were featured in the intervention (see Tables S9 and S10), suggesting that children updated their broader beliefs about communal and agentic careers as a class rather than simply encoding information about the specific careers they saw-some evidence of generalization beyond the specific intervention materials.

Further, our analyses also provide some preliminary evidence that counter-stereotypical exemplars may reduce gender differences in children's own communal values and interest in communal careers. Exploratory analyses suggested that children who saw the counterstereotypical role models (with or without cues that the model had high status) showed reduced gender differences in both communal values and communal career interest, an effect driven predominantly by boys, who showed somewhat stronger affinity toward communal values and careers after the intervention (Table 2, Figures 4 and 5). This pattern is consistent with accounts of role models as "templates" for possible selves: Seeing ingroup members in nontraditional roles provides concrete, positive identity possibilities that children can more readily imagine for themselves (Dasgupta, 2011; Markus & Nurius, 1986; Olsson & Martiny, 2018). Prior rolemodel interventions have primarily targeted gendered interests in STEM (Block et al., 2022; Dasgupta & Asgari, 2004; Gladstone & Cimpian, 2021), with somewhat mixed effects. The current study shows that similar principles can be applied to communion and care-related work, a domain that has been highlighted as important but is under-studied (Croft et al., 2015; Meeussen et al., 2020). As such, our findings provide a proof of concept that counter-stereotypical exemplars can influence children's gender stereotypes and possibly also their own values and career interests in an area with substantial implications for occupational segregation and societal equity.

However, it is also important to note that the effects of the intervention on children's own values and career interests were smaller and not consistently present across outcomes and analyses, suggesting that counter-stereotypical exemplars have a stronger or more immediate effect on how children see the world than on how they see themselves and what they value.

Notably, intervention effects on children's own values and interests were most clearly observed in exploratory, non-preregistered analyses that combined the two counter-stereotypic conditions, which increased our power to detect small effects. The weaker and less consistent evidence for changes in children's own values and interests is reminiscent of past interventions in the math domain, which have been unsuccessful in changing whether children see math as "for me"

despite showing a reduction in both implicit and explicit gender stereotypes about math (Block et al., 2022). These comparatively modest effects are also consistent with the idea that a single brief session is unlikely to counteract years of gender-differentiated socialization, expectations from peers and adults, and structural incentives that favor agentic over communal pursuits for boys.

These forces are part of the social, conformity-based pathway through which stereotypes exert their effects, as discussed in the Introduction. In future research, it may be worthwhile to test role model interventions such as ours as just one component of broader interventions to boost boys' communal values and career interests (Meeussen et al., 2020).

Counter to what we had first theorized, children who viewed counter-stereotypical exemplars who were also described as wealthy and successful did not differ from those who viewed the same counter-stereotypical exemplars without status cues. Thus, although the counter-stereotypical content of the videos has measurable effects, adding status cues did not enhance these effects. This absence of an added status effect is notable in light of prior work showing that adults and older youth typically assign low status to communal values and communal careers (Block, Croft, et al., 2018; Brescoll et al., 2012). Experimental studies with older children and adolescents have also found that salary and power cues can increase interest in occupations, sometimes particularly among boys (Hayes et al., 2018; Korlat et al., 2023; Olsen et al., 2023).

Several non-exclusive explanations are possible. First, even if children registered the wealth cues, they may not have recruited them when judging communal roles as desirable for themselves. School-aged children can track wealth and use it to infer others' status (e.g., Enright et al., 2020; Shutts et al., 2016), but they do not always translate status beliefs into preferences (e.g., Mandalaywala et al., 2020). The wealth cues in our videos may simply not have been weighty enough to shift children's own values and interests. Second, the gender cues in our videos might have overridden the status cues. Gender is often more salient to children than other social categories (Patterson & Bigler, 2006; Shutts, 2015; Taylor et al., 2009). Because the central content of our vignettes involved boys and girls enacting communal or agentic roles, this gendered content may have overshadowed the wealth cues, leaving little room for status to exert an independent effect. Third, the wealth cues may not have been perceived as uniformly positive.

Although salary cues can inform children's career aspirations (Olsen et al., 2023), children also show ambivalence about wealth. For example, 8-to 14-year-olds view high-wealth groups as more selfish (Elenbaas & Killen, 2019) and, with age, increasingly expect these groups to be exclusionary (Burkholder et al., 2020). Future research should examine whether counterstereotypical exemplars are more effective when paired with other status cues, such as prestige or respect.

Developmental Considerations

Exploratory age analyses indicated that the effect of counter-stereotypical role models on gender stereotypes about values and careers was somewhat larger for younger than for older children. Because our intervention used animated, cartoon-like vignettes, it is possible that the materials were more engaging or age-appropriate for younger children than for older children. At the same time, the age effects observed here were modest and not always robust across different outcomes and model specifications. Overall, these patterns are consistent with the idea that beliefs about communion, agency, and status are in flux during early childhood and that this period provides a critical window for interventions (Liben et al., 2001; Martin & Ruble, 2010; Reyes-Jaquez & Koenig, 2022). Future work with broader age ranges should more systematically test whether sensitivity to counter-stereotypical exemplars varies as a function of age and, if so, why.

Limitations and Future Directions

While the current study provides important new evidence that video-based interventions can reduce gender stereotypes about communion and may narrow gender differences in communal values and careers interests, it also has important limitations. First, the longevity of the effects we observed here remains unclear. Related work on the longevity of interventions aimed at reducing implicit racial biases has produced mixed results (Forscher et al., 2019; Lai et al., 2016). Since all outcomes in the current study were assessed immediately after a single intervention session, future work is needed to clarify how durable the effects are.

Second, our sample of children comes from a very specific cultural context, so the results should not be generalized without further testing. Our sample consisted primarily of North American children who had the necessary technology and parental support to participate in an online study. These families may hold relatively egalitarian attitudes, and the baseline level of communal endorsement in this sample was high across conditions. At the same time, North America also has stronger gender stereotypes about communion (Kosakowska-Berezecka et al., 2025) and larger gender differences in communal values and communal career interests than other cultures (Block et al., 2026). It is therefore unclear whether effects of a similar intervention would be smaller or larger in non-Western contexts, requiring more targeted research in the future.

Third, the current study relied on children's explicit self-reports for both their stereotypes and their values and career interests, which warrants caution. Self-reports are typically somewhat skewed toward socially desirable responses. For example, the high ratings for communal values and career interests observed in this sample may reflect social desirability concerns. In addition, despite being based on similar published measures (Block et al., 2026; Olsen et al., 2022), some of our measures (e.g., career interests) showed only modest reliability, which could have masked some effects. To further examine whether the effects of exposure to counter-stereotypical exemplars generalize to less controllable behaviors, future research could incorporate additional, behavioral measures (e.g., a task in which children can opt to play a communal or agentic role) or implicit stereotype measures.

Finally, in future research it would be worthwhile to pursue the practical question of whether interventions should target communal careers broadly or focus on cultivating interest in specific such careers. Our exploratory generalization analyses offer tentative guidance. In our primary models, the intervention's effects were comparable for careers featured in the intervention videos and careers presented only at test, suggesting that exposure to counterstereotypical examples in a small set of careers may be sufficient to shift children's broader beliefs about who values and pursues communal work, as well as their own values and career interests (though the intervention's effects on these outcomes were overall smaller and less consistent). Thus, interventions may not need to list every communal career to be effective; a few vivid, well-chosen exemplars may help move children's general beliefs about communal roles and perhaps also how much they value these roles for themselves.

Conclusion

Communal values and roles are vital for individual well-being and for the functioning of societies, yet boys and men remain underrepresented in communal work and often distance themselves from communion throughout childhood and adolescence. We demonstrate that a brief, theory-driven role-model intervention can reduce children's gender stereotypes about communal values and careers and may also narrow gender differences in boys' and girls' own communal values and communal career interests. By targeting children's gendered beliefs about communion and agency, role-model interventions such as this one are a useful part of broader efforts to promote more gender-balanced engagement in care work across the life span.

Figure 1 Still Frames and Accompanying Script from a Sample Video from Each of The Three Conditions [SOM]).

Whether girl or boy answer options were presented first was randomized within and between participants. Responses were coded such that higher values represent more stereotypical responses. That is, for communal values, responses were coded on a 5-point scale ranging from2 = girls care a lot more to -2 = boys care a lot more, with 0 representing no gender stereotype about communal values. For agentic values, responses were coded on a 5-point scale ranging from2 = boys care a lot more to -2 = girls care a lot more, with 0 representing no gender stereotype about agentic values. Responses were averaged to represent children's stereotypes regarding communal (α = 0.85) and agentic (α = 0.70) values.

The current study tested such an intervention: specifically, a brief, videobased counter-stereotypical role model intervention in which both the children and the experimenters administering the outcome measures were unaware of condition. The intervention reliably weakened children's stereotypes, with more mixed but promising evidence for effects on children's own values and career interests. Because the materials are brief and standardized, this approach is straightforward to administer and potentially scalable.

Note. "Counter" = counter-stereotypical. The measures of gender stereotypes were on a scale from -2 to 2, with 0 as the neutral point, positive values indicating traditional stereotypes (e.g., girls as communal), and negative values indicating nontraditional stereotypes (e.g., boys as communal).
